Municipalities are important. The municipal sector managed on behalf of taxpayers’ annual income of 435.2 billion. kr in 2015. Measured in gross domestic product, the municipal sector generated some 17,5 pst. in both 2013 and 2014. The Committee feels that the municipal sector has an important role to play as a foundation for the Norwegian welfare society, and we would stress the importance of the municipalities' position as the arena for strong local democracy. Local authorities represent both proximity, belongings, and the ability to influence the inhabitants. The community proposition we are dealing with today has a number of themes. Of course, local reform is one of the most important issues this year, and it is now one year since the municipal reform was dealt with in the Stortinget. A large number of them concluded that we should ask the municipalities to sit down and look at their own situation. It is important to note that the municipal reform has not been found in this room or in government offices. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e371	Today, the Stortinget is taking a new step towards a more vibrant local democracy and a more populous and community-friendly municipal sector. And we continue to work on renewing, simplifying and improving the public sector. The government’s 2016 proposals for the local economy generate a growth in the free revenues that are on average at the level of the last few years. The real space for action for municipalities is larger in 2015 than it was in 2014 and the years before, and it gets bigger in 2016 than it is in 2015. At the hearing in the Committee, the KS showed that under the Right-Fremskritspart-government, the announced growth of free revenues available to municipalities is increasing more than under the red-green. So the difference is not primarily in the amount of growth, but in how much of the money local authorities can actually determine the use of themselves. The Right-Fremskritsparte government provides greater space for action to the municipalities when we increase the growth of the free revenues. In the right, we believe it is important that municipalities have their own tax revenues – they can, as we know, fluctuate from one year to another. The alternative is a hundred percent grant-funded local government sector, which I don't think any of us want. However, we are not in principle opposed to making a continuous assessment of the financial situation of the municipalities and adding additional appropriations when needed. Well, we're going to show you that right now. As of 2015, we strengthened the growth grant for the municipality, so that municipalities can better manage the ‘growth pain’ resulting from the rapidly increasing need to expand, among others. school and kindergarten. And precisely stimulating growth – and recognising that we want municipalities that can and will affect their own growth – is important. Therefore, it is important to organise and make growth possible for more. Norway needs a more entrepreneurial policy at local and national level, not tax increases and additional burdens. That's why we're warning that a share of the corporate tax from 2017 is going back to municipalities that have grown in business – whether you had a lot or little business from before. Today, the municipality receives zero dollars of corporate tax. In the Right, we think it's right that a share of the tax is left in municipalities that generate growth. It is very strange to hear red-green politicians argue against the tax because the municipalities obviously cannot influence themselves whether there is private business or not in the municipalities. Yes, they can. It is a gross underestimation of the Municipality of Norway to claim that Norwegian municipalities that have a wide and varied business life have not contributed to this themselves – as if it were just coincidences, happiness and piety that determine whether a municipality is considered to be business-friendly or not. We need business-friendly municipalities, but yes, we need a lot more municipalities that have muscles and skills to do a real job in this area. It is also to strengthen the growth power of the country as a whole that we have a record of traffic and roads, including county roads. Including 2016, we're up in an increase of 1.2 billion. kr to the county roads since the government change. And it's for the same reason – growth power across the country – that we are now introducing a ferry relief system, so that it can become more county road, more county tunnel or more county bridge where today you depend on a county ferry. From before we know that it was this government that provided for the continuation of the interest compensation scheme for transport measures in the counties, a scheme that the red-greens tried to scrape. This also gives more and better way. This is a joyful follow-up to what most parties have also been concerned about in the Municipal Committee. I mention growth subsidies, business-friendly policies, aquaculture communities, corporate taxes and the strengthening of transport measures because, in sum, it shows that we are making it easier and more profitable for municipalities to contribute to something both they themselves and the whole of Norway need more of now: high-growth local communities with a varied and profitable business life, across the country. A business-friendly policy is and will be the most important district policy we can pursue. We still have separate schemes with North Norway grants, district policy instruments and differentiated employer fees. However, Norway as a nation must always have more profits than grants, and then we must have more policies that make it more attractive to organise business and make it easier for business to run economically. Such a policy leads the government. Nearly every five workers in Norway work in the municipal sector. Teachers, nurses, nurses, engineers and fire engineers are examples of occupations that show the enormous breadth of the community with and are responsible for. However, being a good municipality requires more than money. It also requires the municipality to attract, retain and develop skilled employees. At that time, you must be an attractive employer and be able to offer professional environments and career and development opportunities to those who want to do so. In addition, competent managers who cooperate with employees and who make the complex municipal organisation work well together are required to make good offers to the inhabitants and at the same time make best use of the resources available to them. I say this with all possible respect, because driving a municipality is probably one of the most complex things you can be responsible for in the Kingdom. Not everyone is looking at the municipality from the outside who knows the diversity of services, employee groups, citizens, legislation and the environment. The work of the government on a national competence strategy, which is well described in the proposition, is important in view of this. The cooperation parties today also make clear the expectation that all municipalities will take an active part in the reform process. We do not oblige anyone to draw a certain conclusion, but we expect – in fact – that you do not volunteer and stand out from the debate. The municipalities are well running and have good processes and conversations locally, and then it is still a little year until we see most of the answers, probably before the summer of 2016. We have a government that simplifies, renews and improves Norway, which strengthens local democracy and ensures that the state interferes less, that pursues time thieves and pursues a business-friendly policy, which is important at a time when Norway has to move from specialty to conversion.

Municipalities operate within state-sponsored frameworks and have experienced cutbacks in the framework that are not compensated. Then the municipalities have one thing to do: they have to cut the welfare offer to the inhabitants. There are people who are entitled to municipal care services and school places, who pay the price in terms of poorer offers. KS has published information on the situation in the booklet ‘Tax malfunctions — Municipalities and Norwegian economy’. This shows that, in the years from 2004 to 2013, municipalities have achieved a VAT increase in relation to the estimates of 20 billion. kr at current prices. The red-green government contributed to the growth of the local economy by keeping tax receipts in addition to estimates in municipalities. In the last two years, the local government sector has had to cover most of the tax failure through cuts in services or through increased taxes and property taxes. More than ever, municipal property taxes pay for their housing, and the rates increase. This is contrary to the promises made by the Right and the Advance Party in their programmes. Promises that are not followed up in practice are not worth much. In the last two years, the government has carried out a redistribution of municipalities and counties between them, which is very negative for the districts. The regional development funds have been cut by 30 pst. in two years. The decision on new cost keys for the allocation of framework grants to county municipalities has led to the becoming the major losers in North Norway and the coastal regions. In our budget statement, we're correcting some of this. We think it's going to have to add 600 million. to regional development funds for county councils, and that it is necessary to strengthen the appropriations for racial, slip and flood protection. In the committee consultation, we were shown how the new cost keys underfinance the real costs of boating and ferrying in the counties. The centre party believes that the cost keys need to be reviewed, but until it is completed, we will add 200 million. kr on the C-list under free resources. The Centre Party proposed last year to implement a system of holiday-rescue-rescue-rescue-rescue-rescue-rescue-rescue-rescue-rescue-rescue-rescue-rescue-res. By converting the cost keys last year, the counties lost their ability to finance the bridge or tunnel that replaced the ferry, with provision from their own framework appropriations. Now a joint committee follows. That's good, but the Center Party wants to see if this system should also apply to boat routes. The conversion of corporate tax, where municipalities will receive a new income based on local value creation, will take money from most municipalities and give to a few. The centre party has led the Ministry of Finance to calculate the effects. They were discouraging. 40 municipalities win, and 388 municipalities lose. It's probably the sheriff of Nottingham and not Robin Hood who's the government's ideal in the distribution policy. Last year, the growth grant increased by 330 million. kr. Municipalities with low growth or population decline paid the entire bill. The centre party was in favour of increasing the growth grant, but added it as an increased framework. The conversion of corporation tax has a much larger bill, about Three billion. kr, sent to Kommune-Norge. Communes of commuters in central districts and the vast majority of districts were cut into their borders. They are told by the government to reduce their welfare benefits so that municipalities that are better placed to strengthen their offerings. The Centre Party proposes that the Government should delete the proposal for the conversion of corporate tax. The scheme presented by the government to stimulate private value creation in municipalities also has another effect that is not described, but which they may consider to be a positive side effect. When municipalities are to receive back some of the local value creation based on the salary base of the local business community, this means that additional municipal benefits are granted to those who privatise care for the elderly, school and kindergarten. Finally, I would like to address the proposal that the Central Party has put forward, and the ones that we are with others about.

And it's not least about how the municipalities evolve as the arena of local democracy and as employers. I am pleased that the population survey now presented shows that the population is more satisfied with the municipal services today than they were two years ago. I also notice that the total population is the most satisfied in the municipalities that have more than 20 000 inhabitants. In order to achieve the key objectives of the local government sector in the future, we need to ensure that municipalities have a good and predictable framework and that resources are fairly distributed throughout the country. With the income framework we are notifying for the municipal sector next year, we have arranged for municipalities to continue to develop their welfare offerings to their inhabitants. We're notifying a growth in the total revenue of 6-7 billion. kr and a growth in the free income of between 4,5 and 5 billion. kr. Consequently, the growth of the free revenues is in line with the average growth in recent years. However, if we are to get an overall picture of the actual economic situation in the municipalities, we need to look at more factors than the percentage of income growth. It is also about the development of costs. Both in 2014 and 2015, we see that different elements are moving in different directions. Tax revenue is weaker than intended, but we also see that expenditure, among other things. for remuneration and demographic development are lower than expected. The government has, following an overall assessment, proposed to strengthen the municipalities' framework by 1.1 billion. kr in the revised national budget. After the negotiations with the Christian People's Party, this has increased to 1.2 billion. kr. I notice that the Workers' Party, which throughout this spring has blackened the situation in Kommune-Norway and talked about the need for cutbacks in the local economy, actually proposes to allocate less money to the municipalities than the bourgeois parties do for 2015. That means that the credibility of the party that has tried to blackpaint is not very great. We also continue the practice that has had broad political outreach, namely that there should be no automatic compensation for tax failures, but that a concrete assessment of the overall economic situation in the municipal sector should be made. According to KS, the real economic space in the municipalities for 2014–2016 is as good as the three previous years under the Stoltenberg government. The government believes that it is necessary to improve the provision of drug users and mental health challenges. We strengthen national offers and give local communities room for increased bets. Within the free incomes, we have provided leadership to ensure that municipalities give priority to school health services and improved provision in the field of drug and mental health. We did it in 2014, we do it in 2015, and we propose it for 2016. Now we expect the municipalities to follow up and give a lift to people who are struggling. However, although the financial framework for the municipal sector is good, it will still be necessary to make demanding priorities. It is still necessary to work for resources to be used in an even better way. That's the situation today, as it was in the last eight years. Last year, we updated the cost keys of county municipalities and made sure that they reflect more closely the actual expenditure of county municipalities on public transport, secondary schools and other services. After years of deferral of conversion, we are now in the process of introducing a more equitable system. We know it involves conversions in some counties, but the conversion phase is phased in gradually and with a good transitional arrangement. However, it is important to bear in mind that for many years, counties such as Rogaland, West Agder, South Trondelag, Buskerud, Oslo, Westfold and More and Romesdal have received significantly lower transfers than the needs of these counties. When the red-greens speak out critically about the conversion we have implemented, they should remember that it is the inhabitants of these counties who have paid the price for the necessary changes that have been put at risk for a long time. The conversion we're doing now will give these states a better chance to offer their inhabitants a more equivalent offer. One of the main areas of government activity is building the country. Through a sharp increase in investment in road, rail and public transport, we ensure that people can get home safely and that goods can arrive quickly. The appropriations for county roads have increased by more than 1 billion. NOK since 2013, and we announce a further increase next year. This increase is an important part of the government's regional and regional policy. I am also pleased that the Stortinge joins the government's proposal for a ferry redemption scheme for the county roads. This will allow many counties to replace ferries with bridges, roads and tunnels. This important reform will make a major difference in the daily lives of both peoples and businesses in the districts. Many of the prominent representatives of the Progress Party, the Christian People's Party, the Left and the Right have insisted on this arrangement being implemented. By agreement with the bourgeois parties, it is extended to 40 years, and of course the government will follow up. It is also important to note that considerable work has been done to update the cost keys for boat and ferry, and the result is that the keys capture the real costs for this purpose. However, we also see that there is still a need to work on, and improve, the system. The size limits set for municipalities today are not just important to allocate values and resources. It is also important to facilitate the contribution of municipalities to creating values and jobs. I am pleased that the majority at Stortinget supports the proposal to allow municipalities to retain a greater proportion of the values created locally, through the return of parts of the corporate tax. The model we're designing will give all municipalities an opportunity to get a share of this income. When we base income on growth in wages and salaries, municipalities get a motivation to better facilitate growth. Instead of rewarding old industrial structures, municipalities are rewarded for new jobs. It is particularly important at a time of transition to the Norwegian economy, where many have to find new industries to live off. At the same time, the scheme we are proposing is moderate, and it will therefore also have moderate redistribution effects. All the pieces are not in place in the new scheme. The aim is for employees to spend less time on unnecessary reporting and bureaucratic processes and more time providing better and faster assistance to their citizens. And we'll make sure there's enough caring employees to give kids a real deal when their parents are at work. It's good for the kids, it's good for the parents, and it's good for the country.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e1319	Norway was forced to liquidate the differentiated employer’s fee scheme on 1 January 2004, but the Stoltenberg government was able to reintroduce the scheme from 1 January 2007. Differentiating employers’ fees means that employers pay different rates in different places in the country, depending on population density and some other objective factors. My concern is that today's government does not seem to prioritise the task of continuing reduced rates and compensation for Bodø and Tromsø. Bodø and Tromsø are special in North Norway, at a higher rate than the rest of the country, although both cities are located in the instrument area. The normal rate for the employer’s fee in Norway is 14.1 p.m., while the rate for Northern Norway in general is 5.1 p.m. or lower. For Bodø and Tromsø's part, the rate is 7.9 pst. This difference is also the basis for the compensation scheme. The reason why Bodø and Tromsø have received a bet between the person in the instrument area and the ordinary rate is that, on the one hand, these cities do not have the same challenges as the rest of District Norway, while on the other hand they do not get a full bet, since they are regional drivers of development and value creation. However, as the employment situation now develops for the private sector in Bodø and Tromsø, with stagnation and partly negative developments, the focus on the role of these cities needs to be maintained. The compensation scheme for paid employers’ contributions has contributed to positive development over a number of years. I would particularly like to point out large investments in knowledge institutions and infrastructure, funds that are available in close cooperation with industry and the chosen level. These are investments that have benefited the regions, highlighting the importance of these two cities for the rest of the country. The regionally elected level also has a special significance in North Norway, since they represent competences and weights that are important in a part of the country with scattered settlements and many small municipalities. In my view, these two cities, together with the county councils in North Norway, have unique roles. They represent driving forces and competences that represent a significant weight, and they are resources for further development and growth. If we are to take the employment situation seriously, the two cities must have framework conditions to ensure that they can perform their roles as drivers of positive population development and value creation in the north. I would therefore like to stress the importance of maintaining the system of compensation for a differentiated employer’s fee in the future and of transferring the compensation to the respective county municipalities. Responsibility for the continuation and security of the system now rests on the Right and the Advance Party of the Government. It worries me. That is why, in conclusion, I would like to say what Mr Gudmundsen said in the introduction: 'We are going to have an exciting meeting'.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e1338	From 1 July 2014, 31 new municipalities joined the differentiated employer’s fee scheme. This was presented as a great novelty from the government, although the conversion also resulted in important sectors such as transport being excluded from the scheme. In my home state, the municipality of the West was one of those who looked forward to positive effects of the conversion, but that was not the case. The proceeds of the fees charged to the 31 municipalities were estimated at 267 million. kr. But the government didn't want it to be as well, because in the municipal proposition it's called: ‘Different employer’s fee is a scheme aimed at the business sector in the districts and is intended to facilitate employment growth throughout the country. The scheme shall be neutral for the municipal sector.’» The 31 municipalities that received a fee were therefore deducted for 2015 from the corresponding amount in the calculation of the framework fee. Some even doubled, because the compensation they had received for previous changes in the differentiated employer fee was withdrawn. Some municipalities, therefore, were drawn from the department as well as from the county. These municipalities received a reduction in their income. In the municipal bill this year, the government is doing a partial retreat. Now the government wants a gradual degression over five years of the extraction from the framework. This cannot be understood in any other way than that the government here acknowledges errors with these 31 municipalities. I think there is reason to be strongly critical of the uncertainty the government has created for the municipalities concerned. The municipalities thought they would get a tax return of 267 million. kr, but felt that they had lost everything – some of them more. There is no reason why the unfair move they were subjected to last year should be corrected over five years. The Central Party, together with the Socialist Left Party, has therefore put forward a proposal asking the Government to correct the move to these 31 municipalities in the 2016 state budget. This proposal should be given a number if we are to deal with the relevant municipalities correctly. The centre party has included compensation in our 2016 proposal for a framework. So the second one, and perhaps the most important part of it, remains, which is also taken up in the proposal from us and SV. The effect of differentiated employer charges is not included in the income system for municipalities. The biggest concern is that the reduction in the cost of labour is a district policy tool that benefits both business and the public sector. This commitment has not been supported by the Ministry's formulation that it should be neutral for the municipal sector. The government is well aware that the framework of the 31 municipalities was exposed last year was groundless. Now it remains that the government acknowledges that both private and municipal businesses should benefit from the effects of a differentiated employer’s fee.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e1824	The Left takes with it an important initiative for a debate on post-preservation and initiatives to strengthen child-restraint protection. The representative proposal has been thoroughly and thoroughly reviewed. Proposalers describe today’s situation and challenges in the field of after-protection in a very good way. Young people receiving assistance from child protection services before the age of 18 are currently not entitled to child protection support after the age of 18 years, but child protection has a duty to assess the need for assistance and support from young people. Section 1-3 of the Children’s Protection Act states that measures taken before the child reaches the age of 18 may be maintained at the age of 23 if he/she agrees. If the young people say no, they have a right of withdrawal and can contact them after the connection with child protection has been formally concluded. If the child protection service terminates the measure at the age of 18, this shall be justified in the best interests of the child. Young people who are denied the right to apply for the protection of their children are entitled to apply for legal redress. From 2009 onwards, the law has been somewhat strengthened as regards the possibility of obtaining post-protection measures for young people with child protection experience, but it is weak that the burden of proof for justifying action lies with young people themselves. The proposers believe that the legal protection of young people and the possibility of posterity are too weak, and the Christian People's Party shares this assessment. Research supports increasing the likelihood of mastering the transition to adult life if young people are given good and proper post-protection measures. As pointed out in the proposal and the submission, the National Association for Children’s Protection, Children’s Protection Professionals, researchers and many young people with child protection experience believe that after-care is not working properly enough and that in practice many young people do not receive the support and assistance they need. Many have also not been able to build lasting relationships, which are an important foundation for mastering adult life. Proposalers are very concerned that after-protection is an area of child protection that is deprived, despite the fact that too many are well protected after-care as a prerequisite for success in adult life. The Christian People's Party also shares this concern. The Christian People's Party has programmed that we want to secure child protection children who need it, after protection up to 23 years. We agree with the rapporteurs that there is a need for a strong commitment to post-closure and that the provision of post-closure protection should also be able to be provided until young people are 25 years old. The Christian People's Party agrees that there is a need to strengthen the law. It is essential for young people with child protection experience to be guaranteed good child protection measures even after the age of 18 years, and this is not an institutionalised practice today. Proposalers believe that the Stortinget is responsible for ensuring this both by strengthening legislation and legislation and by ensuring that child protection is provided with sufficient resources and competences to carry out and ensure active post-protection work on the basis of the needs of young people and on the premises of young people. The proposers believe that the public should have special responsibility for young adults where the child protection service has been in the place of their parents. The Christian People's Party fully agrees and supports this. However, we do not support paragraph 1 of the draft representative on the right to action until the age of 25. With regard to the second proposal, on control and supervision, therefore, an overall committee asks that post-protection should be considered as a matter of country-wide supervision as soon as possible. There is clearly a need for both social and child protection services to build on a holistic approach to family, networking, housing and school to find a common understanding with young people, and for the youth to be actively involved in the provision of post-protection measures. In the view of the proposers, there is a need for an action plan with clear and concrete measures to strengthen the follow-up of young people with child protection experience after the age of authority. The majority of the population will assess the need for an action plan on post-hoc protection when the Storting is about to deal with the home announcement or the changes to the Child Protection Act. The Christian People's Party believes that there is a need for an action plan and therefore supports this proposal in its vote: ‘The government is asked to provide an action plan for the after-care of young people with child protection experience, emphasising the need for resources, interaction and professional soundness.’ I bring up the proposal that we have made on the matter.

Under the Child Protection Act, municipalities have a statutory obligation to exercise internal control in the area of child protection. The purpose of the internal control is to ensure that the municipalities carry out the tasks assigned to them by law. It is the State Health Authority that has overall professional responsibility for the supervision of child protection, and it is also those who decide what should be the theme of nationwide supervision. But I can assure Stortinget that in the dialogue with the Health Authority, it will be discussed whether it is appropriate to exercise better supervision against the protection of the individual municipality. In the light of these initiatives mentioned here, I do not see the need to draw up a specific action plan.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e1990	First of all, I would like to thank the representatives of the Workers' Party who are promoting this proposal on quality in the crisis centre offer. This is a very important theme, and the proposal testifies to a commendable commitment by these representatives. As the rapporteur on the subject, I would like to explain the background and content of the proposal and the Committee's views and position before, as the Christian People's Party's representative in the Committee, I present the views of the Christian People's Party. The other parties shall make their views known. The responsibility for the crisis centre offer was transferred to the municipalities from 2010, and the Stortinget agreed at the same time that the crisis centre offer should be a statutory task for Norwegian municipalities. The Norwegian Institute for Growth, Welfare and Ageing, NOVA, has carried out an evaluation of the implementation of the Emergency Centre Act by municipalities, following a mandate from the Directorate for Children, Youth and Family Affairs. The mission was to assess whether the crisis centre offer in the municipalities has developed in line with the law’s requirements after the introduction of the crisis centre law. The main conclusion of the evaluation is that most municipalities now contribute to the financing of a crisis facility for both men and women, as required by law. NOVA writes in its report that the main picture is that municipalities are seeking to meet the requirements of the Crisis Centre Act by taking over or connecting to existing crisis centres. At the same time, the evaluation shows challenges and objectives that do not appear to have been achieved. Reference is made to challenges related to the provision of services by municipalities to groups with additional problems, that municipalities are not in the process of coordinating relief measures for victims in a coherent chain of action, and that the responsibilities of the re-establishment phase are unclear. The evaluation also shows that many municipalities lack action plans to work against violence in close relationships. Information on crisis centres is also missing. This is the background for the proposals discussed in this setting. Proposalers are committed to ensuring the quality of the crisis centre offer, to be provided in all municipalities of the country, for all citizens, and promote 18 proposals to strengthen supply across the country. In its letter of reply, the Council of State points out that the work on evaluating the recommendations of the evaluation report, presented on 2 February of this year, has started but has not been completed. On this basis, the Council of State gives its preliminary assessments and comments on the proposals. In the opinion, the Committee notes that we share the intentions of the proposers to ensure good content and quality in the crisis centre offer in all municipalities of the country, for all citizens. The Committee concludes that the draft representative is annexed to the Minutes. The Labour Party supports 14 proposals in the vote. For the Christian People’s Party, it is important to point out that the NOVA evaluation report shows that there are a number of challenges and objectives that have not been achieved within today’s crisis centre offer. These are deficiencies and needs that need to be corrected and followed up. When the Council of State in its letter of reply informs us that she has started to evaluate the report's recommendations, we in the Christian People's Party welcome that the government will come to Stortinget with proposals on how to follow up the report once these assessments have been carried out.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e2085	I would like to thank the proposers for an important commitment to a very important topic. Crisis centres are an important part of the work against violence in close relationships. The Crisis Centres Act was an important milestone in strengthening the provision of assistance to those at risk of violence in an acute crisis. I can assure the proposers that the government is very concerned about ensuring good content and quality in the crisis centre offer. I am therefore pleased that the evaluation of the crisis centre offer, which I myself have repeatedly requested in the opposition and in the position, shows that we are on the right track. Nevertheless, the evaluation points to clear challenges. The users of crisis centres in Norway are satisfied with the assistance they receive and the provision of children has improved. The competence of staff at the crisis centres has improved. The majority of centres now have their own children’s professional responsibility, and most employees at the centres have higher education. There is evidence of increased professionalisation. Most municipalities now have a crisis centre offer for men. It was far from the case before the law. However, there are clear gaps in how municipalities care for violent men and their accompanying children. The same applies to the offer to those at risk of violence with drug problems or serious mental health problems. The offer at many centres is also not universally designed. Local authorities lack action plans and are not in the process of coordinating relief measures. Many of the proposals under consideration today involve a stronger state regulation of the content and quality of the crisis centre offer, as well as child-care skills at crisis centres. These are proposals I will consider in depth against other alternatives, such as the preparation of circulars or professional guides. Increased state detail management could limit municipal variation and weaken municipal autonomy. To ensure the safety of the users of the crisis centre offer, I consider it so fundamental that I have commissioned Bufdir to prepare a regulation on physical security of the premises. The Directorate is reviewing the supply of children at crisis centres, including children’s rights to help with other services. This will be an important basis for further developing the provision of children in crisis centres. I would like to stress that we are following closely the development of the crisis centre offer both in terms of quality and content, and in terms of geographical coverage of the offer. Some small crisis centres have been closed since the law came into force, but the number of fixed sites on a national basis has increased. Larger and more robust centres can help improve the quality of the offer. It is the responsibility of municipalities to coordinate measures between the crisis centre and other services. The law is in place, but we know that good coordination is difficult to put into practice. Local action plans against violence in close relationships can be a useful tool to ensure coordinated assistance for victims of violence. I would encourage several municipalities to draw up action plans, but it should nevertheless be up to each municipality to assess the need for an action plan. The State should not control in detail the tools to be used by municipalities in their work. As regards the physical standard of some crisis centres, improvements are needed. However, after visiting a number of crisis centres in recent years, I am optimistic. Many municipalities have, through good cooperation, succeeded in establishing good offers within the current budgetary framework. I am concerned that the crisis centre offer for additional vulnerable groups is not good enough. Municipalities have a responsibility to provide all persons with close relations with an offer of protection against violence, regardless of additional issues. There is a need to consider further how the State can assist municipalities in meeting these challenges. Finally, I would like to point out that it is County that supervises how municipalities follow up the crisis centre law. I will carry out an assessment of how supervision works and, if necessary, whether it needs to be strengthened. A good offer of crisis centres is crucial for families in crisis living with violence.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e2403	A broad majority of cases joined the criteria for digitisation of the national radio network in 2011. On the basis of professional assessments by the National Communications Authority and the Media Authority, the government concluded on 16 April this year that the criteria have been met and that the national radio network can be digitised during 2017. Norway is thus the first country in the world to implement the technology change. Norway is the first for several reasons: firstly, the FM network is very expensive to operate in a long-term mountainous region with scattered housing patterns; secondly, in Norway, we have a radio market characterised by good competition, high innovation and high profitability. The radio medium is growing, and Norwegian media barometer numbers show that the Norwegian people have not heard so much on radio since 1994. However, one of the key assumptions has been that we have achieved unique cooperation on digitisation between all actors in the nationwide radio market. Here P4, Radio Norway and the NRK have come together. The fact that digitisation has been possible is also due to the trust between market actors and policy authorities. The digital radio communication established clear criteria and a clear plan for implementing the technology change. This has enabled actors to make large investments in confidence that Norwegian politicians keep their word. In a media reality where the radio media is challenged from music streaming, podcasting and other digital services, digitisation is a major investment in the radio media, and it shows that the market believes that radio will continue to be a very important medium in the future. In the digital radio communication, it was assumed that most and least local radio operators would have longer time to make the technology change, but that larger commercial local radios would make the technology change at the same time as Radio Norway and P4. Instead of specifying the categories of local radios to continue on FM, the digital radio communication made it possible for the Ministry to return to Stortinget in 2015. I am pleased that a large majority of the family and cultural committee have joined the call for proposals on the framework conditions for local radio in connection with the digitisation of the radio media, and that a large majority have thereby ensured that the latest political clarification for the technology shift in 2017 is in place. The technology change criteria are met. If they had not, all the concessions had to be renewed from 2017. Of a total of 238 local radios, only 23 channels – the metropolitan radios in the four largest cities – are not offered for extension in line with Stortingen’s 2011 decision. 22 of these 23 major urban radios are already transmitting on DAB today, at prices similar to those in the FM network. The last radio will also be able to send on the DAB network. Therefore, after the technology change has been completed, metropolitan radios release costs for double distribution. There are several advantages to the shift. In the FM network there is room for a handful of local radios in each area, while in DAB there will be space for between 15 and 20 local radios that transmit 24 hours a day in each region. The increased diversity of the network allows for significant liberalisation of the framework conditions for the local radio sector. Regular application rounds with so-called beauty contests are replaced by a single registration system. The content requirements imposed on the Authority have been deleted. This means removing barriers to establishment, less red tape, lower operating costs for the radios and that operators in the radio industry can have a full focus on doing what they are best at, creating good radio content. I stress that no local radio concessionaries are being deprived of anything good as a result of digitisation. In order to provide the local radio sector outside major cities with longer time to implement the technology change, 215 of the total 238 local radios are offered free concessional extensions on FM for five years. In addition, the Communication provides for a significant liberalisation of the framework conditions of the FM. The purpose of this is not to apply more stringent rules to the local radios in the FM network than to local radios in the DAB network. In addition, the Government makes provision to earmark about half of the funds from this support scheme for the digitisation of the local radio sector.

The criterion of population coverage implies that the NRK has to reach the same number of DABs as P1 is currently doing on FM. Then the commercial radio DAB network must reach at least 90 pst. by the population. This corresponds to the coverage requirement of the Radio Norway FM concession. Population coverage is based on household coverage. But it's not just coverage, as it might have been indicated here earlier; it's household coverage, and it's also the FM network today based on. DAB coverage is compared to reception of FM in stereo. It is therefore possible that for some listeners — who have received weak FM signals that have only given the possibility of receiving in mono after the closure — there has been some noise.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e2603	The representatives Toppe and Schlagsvold Vedum promote representative proposals where the main intention is to stop plans for the co-location of hospital functions in a large hospital in Gaustad and re-open Aker Hospital as a fully-fledged local hospital. A unanimous committee indicates that the combination of Health-East and Health-South and the transition that followed was complicated and demanding. The aim of the merger was to improve the coordination of regional enterprises with professional personnel, the correct use of land, medical devices, technology and support functions. The National Audit Office’s reports, revisions and control of the company and the process have repeatedly confirmed that no financial framework was taken into account, that hospitals were scattered in Oslo, that hospitals had different ICT solutions, and that some changes depended on others being carried out. Health South-East has started a number of projects to evaluate the process. They have also started work on identifying the capacity needs of Oslo and Akershus in the short and long term. Oslo University Hospital is now implementing the project Idéphase Oslo University Hospital to provide a good basis for further development of the hospital by 2030. The work includes the need to upgrade construction and plans for new construction. The results of both these efforts shall be presented to the Governing Boards in December 2015. The majority of the Committee believes that it is premature to conclude on concrete solutions and that it is not right to start with appropriations for unexplored projects, as the Socialist Left wishes. It creates precedent and distorts the whole process of decision-making on hospital financing. The majority also believe that it is not right to intervene in investigation processes, as the Center Party and the Socialist Left Party want. It is not right to override the responsibility assigned to Oslo University Hospital. The processes shall be able to provide the professional decision-making basis for future decisions, and it is wrong, in the opinion of the majority, to intervene and override the responsibilities of the hospital in fact. This can have significant professional and economic consequences that no one can see at this time. It is recorded that an alternative with a collection of Oslo University Hospitals on one area after a consultation round has been removed, and the majority of the Committee is satisfied with that. Without now considering different alternatives, a partial collection of hospital functions in the south of Gaustad combined with local hospitals appears more appropriate than the original option. It has been raised beyond any doubt that the building stock in large parts of Oslo University Hospital is not appropriate, and it is necessary to invest in new land in order to be able to operate modern. The Idea phase will provide Oslo University Hospital with a basis for the overall development of the enterprise in the future, and it is positive, the Committee believes, that the organisation of the cancer area should also be investigated. It is realistic to imagine that development will take place in stages with several individual projects that need to be planned within a comprehensive framework and direction. The economic impact of the different options is important. The surveys carried out by Oslo University Hospital show that the continuation of operations at today's locations is the most expensive option. For the purpose of assessing the capacity needs of Oslo and Akershus, the majority of the Committee is committed to assessing short- and medium-term measures ensuring the safety and predictability of the hospital offer and to using available capacity on Aker, Diakonhjemmet and Lovisenberg. The Committee is aware that assessments of the future local hospital offer are part of the idea phase of Oslo University Hospital and that the use of Aker Hospital will also be considered. The council of state has previously said in Stortinget that there has not been a performance in not using Aker hospitals. Finally, I would like to stress that for a long time in the future, operations will take place in today's existing hospital. It is absolutely necessary to establish a sound decision-making basis with sound anchoring both internally within the organisation and among external stakeholders for the further development of Oslo University Hospital.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e2678	In 2001, the state assumed hospital responsibilities — with corporate governance in five health regions. It should improve coordination of the professional and research environment and ensure the use of personnel, area, equipment, technology and, in particular, support functions. This change has been demanding, especially for hospitals. It has also shown that there are not always large companies and large hospitals that are the best. This has been treated many times in this hall. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e2856	We've been in hospital for years in this country. This has been a successful model, especially in regions with large distances. Hospitals have provided health services to the population and the development of good cooperation with hospitals and other health services. Research shows that hospitals have lower consumption of hospital services than districts without such services. A study conducted by researchers Erik Langfeldt and Ivar Johannes Aaraas from 2000 documents that hospitals replace hospitalization for very many patients, and allows others to be printed out earlier. In the four municipalities of Nord-Troms, Kåfjord, Kvänangen, Skjervøy and Nordreisa, the Health Nord regional health company has financed nine hospitals since 2005. The municipalities together have over 11,000 inhabitants and are a large region geographically. The distance from the northernmost municipality of Kvænangen to the nearest hospital in Tromsø is the equivalent of the route Oslo-Kristiansand, and the region has a challenging infrastructure with rasable distances and often winter-bound roads. Hospitals in Nord-Troms have always had good covering on their beds, and all municipalities have stable and competent health professionals. Figures from the municipalities show that a large proportion of those admitted to hospitals can be sent to their own homes after treatment, without having to be treated in a hospital. However, through the implementation of the cooperation reform, there was a lack of clarity about the financing of hospitals in Nord-Troms, which, unlike the Finnish hospitals, have had a purely regional grant. In 2013, the health company announced that it believed that the Interaction Reform would gradually make hospitals redundant. The working party believes that hospitals are an important complement to other health services, especially in such regions as Nord-Troms. Therefore, in the 2014 budget, the red-green government placed a clear order with the healthcare company that hospitals in Nord-Troms should continue to have funding from the health company’s budget. That order has not been followed up by the current government. That is why there is now a pressure on the funding of hospitals. Fortunately, in the spring, the health company has announced to the four hospitals that it will continue the current funding scheme in 2016, but considers it a premise that all beds in the region should be co-located to the District Medical Centre in the largest municipality, Nordreisa. A collection of resources can, of course, be right in many places and contribute to better services to the population. But the four hospital municipalities in North Troms are clear that they want to continue the distribution of beds you have today. It is the municipalities that have the health care responsibility for the hospital beds and for the skills needed. Then it must also be that the municipalities should have the last word in terms of the kind of organisation they want for the beds. The Workers’ Party, together with the SV, the Christian People’s Party and the Centre, promotes a proposal that the health company must continue to invest in hospitals, as they have announced, and that it should be possible to do so on the basis of a model that the municipalities themselves find useful and which at the same time provides the best services to the people of the region. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e2874	The organisation of specialist healthcare in Norway needs to take a lot of account. There are geographical considerations, population base, specific regional conditions and not least the competences available. In long-distance districts, it is important that good and appropriate solutions are developed between health businesses, hospitals and municipalities. District Medical Centres such as Hallingdal Sickestugu in Buskerud, Valdre’s local medical centre in Uppland and North-Troms hospitals are examples of this. The specialist healthcare service finances such district health services in different ways. Often it is that the municipalities agree to have a so-called host municipality for one centre. It has been demonstrated that residents in long-distance districts to hospitals benefit very well from these offers. What we are dealing with today is financing nine beds in Nord-Troms, which is financed by Health Nord, and there are nine beds distributed among the four municipalities of Skjervøy, Nordreisa, Kåfjord and Kvänangen. I am pleased that, in a letter to the Committee, the Health Minister informs me that there are no plans to weaken the funding offer for hospital operations in Nord-Troms in 2016. As of 1 January 2016, all municipalities in the country will have a statutory obligation to have municipal immediate help beds. These are funded through a framework transfer to municipalities. This is part of the continuation of the cooperation reform, which is also the case in Nord-Troms. These beds are therefore to be considered a municipal responsibility. The objectives of the Interaction Reform are to link services in hospitals and municipalities. The budget of the Ministry of Health and Welfare has been transferred to the budget of the Ministry of Local Affairs and the Ministry of Modernisation. In addition, the Committee has been informed, by letter from Health Nord to the municipalities, that it has decided to continue the financing of hospital operations beyond this transfer. This means that the economic transfers do not change. We are committed to ensuring the further operation of the health service, and that residents of Skjervøy, Nordreisa, Kåfjord and Kvänangen should have a good local health service. The Right Party, the Advance Party and the Left therefore promote proposals in the case, that the government must ensure that the inhabitants of these municipalities receive equivalent specialist health services and that further cooperation is facilitated. We know that there are different opinions between the municipalities and Health Nord about how to organise the offer, and that it is a wish from the company that the beds are located at Sonjatun in Nordreisa. Now it is not surprising that a health company believes anything about the use of their money in municipalities. Telemedicine can provide good follow-up even with long distances and closed roads. It also requires the development of good competence in telemedicine in the four municipalities concerned. In autumn 2015, the government presented a national health and hospital plan for the Stortinget. The plan will be the government's tool for setting up hospitals and specialist health services in the future. We have therefore highlighted in the decision the importance of the government’s description of the forms and development of interaction between municipalities and specialist health services – precisely how specialist health services should interact. This involves looking at the national health and hospital level in the context of ongoing work in the Ministry of Health and Welfare affecting the municipalities. This applies, for example: the public health, primary health, the upcoming quality and patient safety reporting and the notified escalation plan for the drug field. There is therefore no stop in financing the specialist health services in Nord-Troms.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e2953	Hospitals in Nord-Troms are an example of an offer the government will have more of as a follow-up to the Cooperation Reform. Providing health services outside hospitals where individuals live is good for the individual, and it is a good social economy. The organisation and financing of healthcare in Nord-Troms must be agreed locally between the municipalities concerned and Health Nord. Hospitals are included in this. It is natural that further grants from Health Nord to the operation of hospitals are seen in the context of the grant for setting up new daily services in municipalities in connection with the Cooperation Reform and, where appropriate, other grants. As part of the Interaction Reform, funds will be transferred to the municipal framework in connection with the establishment of new immediate assistance daily services that municipalities have been able to offer from 2012 and which will become a statutory task from 2016. This means that funds that have so far come from hospitals are transferred to the municipality framework from 2016. The aim is that the new offer should help patients to be treated more closely where they live instead of having to be hospitalised, provided that they receive at least an equal offer. In addition, Health Nord has decided to continue the financing of hospital operations in addition to the funds now transferred to the municipality framework, also for 2016. Thus, the financial framework for the health provision of the population of the municipalities concerned will not change as a result of the 2016 conversion. Further development and any adjustments in the hospital provision must be done locally based on knowledge of local circumstances. Health Nord and the municipalities will have to engage in dialogue on how to organise the offer in the context of the support the enterprise will contribute, in addition to the funds now transferred to the municipality framework. Good cooperation between municipalities and specialist health services is important in order to develop good quality services tailored to the needs of the population. An appropriate solution must be based on a real dialogue between the municipalities concerned and Health Nord and the need to assess alternatives to the co-location of supply in the dialogue. The municipalities of Nord-Troms and Health Nord need to clarify whether they see a common advantage of offering in addition to that which helps fewer patients to travel all the way to Tromsø, and whether the municipalities ensure good quality of their services. We must have a health and care service that ensures equal access for the entire population to sound services. Such an offer can only be realised through close cooperation between municipalities and hospitals. Interaction reform with emphasis on continuous treatment processes continues to apply. One of the main objectives of the Interaction Reform is to link services in hospitals and municipalities. Grants from the State budget for the operation of hospitals in Nord-Troms are not planned. The grant of the State budget to hospitals in Finnmark has historical reasons and emerges from a pilot scheme with activity-based funding of hospitals in Finnmark in the 2000s. As a result of the financial instruments of the Cooperation Reform, it was no longer considered appropriate to continue such a pilot scheme and the experiment has therefore been concluded. However, the State budget subsidy was continued. In autumn 2015, the government presented a national health and hospital plan for the Stortinget. The plan will be the government's main tool for setting up hospitals and specialist health services in the future. The national health and hospital plan shall become the government's operational tool for meeting the future need for specialist health services. The plan is drawn up on the basis of what patients and users think healthcare should look like. It shall give concrete content to the concept of ‘patient’s healthcare’. Both process and plan shall reflect user experience, competence and views. By providing a national health and hospital plan, the government will ensure that overall policy decisions on hospitals and specialist health services are taken by the Stortinget. National health and hospital plans are drawn up and seen in conjunction with other ongoing work in the ministry, such as: the notification of primary health, which has now been transmitted to the Stortinget, and the forthcoming notification of quality and patient safety, the alerting of the escalation plan for the drug field and the work currently under way in the prehospital services.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e3057	In North Norway we have a very long and proud tradition of hospital beds. In most coastal municipalities in the north, the development of this high level of competence in the field has resulted, together with the reform of cooperation now demanding that all municipalities have such KAD beds, that these municipalities have a special advantage and prove to be particularly skilled in the field. Indeed, they provide high-quality services and have saved many a hospitalisation, which has provided an increased quality of life for individuals and has also been a good social economy. Over the years, additional skills and opportunities have also been added at the intersection of this two-way service with the specialist healthcare service. In our community and outside hospitals, telemedicine and the development of district medical centres have strengthened the quality of service provision. Furthermore, the development and upgrading of the road network has also reduced distances, and in addition to the professionalisation of the ambulance service, this has actually resulted in a change from transport to a prehospital service along our roads. In sum, this provides security in everyday life for the many who still have a long distance from a hospital, and in a society where we are still demanding high standards of health and safety. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e3276	First of all, I would like to thank the Committee for its good work and for the fact that, in the vote, we are in fact some cross-political about the extent of alcohol damage and the need for a joint alcohol policy to reduce alcohol-related harm and alcohol abuse, which also includes the representative proposal. According to the World Health Organization, alcohol is one of the three main causes of poor health and premature death in Europe. In the public health report, the government has agreed to reduce the harmful use of alcohol by at least 10 pst. by 2025. A joint committee emphasises that a reduction in alcohol damage depends on our efforts to reduce the accumulation of alcohol consumption in the population and that empirical data show covariance between alcohol consumption per capita and the number of people who have harmfully high consumption. It is therefore necessary, the Committee believes, to maintain the objective of low average consumption of alcohol in the population while at the same time putting the search light on the drinking pattern and on third-party damage. It seems clear that alcohol abuse has a very high cost for individuals, for society and for working life. In this motion for a resolution, the Christian People's Party has put forward 15 concrete proposals. None of the multiple numbers are given in the option, even if many parties share the intention behind several of the suggestions. For the Central Party's part, I would like to make it clear that, when it comes to the proposal for maximum time for donation in the municipalities, we have a program of expansion that requires the maximum time to be at 0 p.m. 03, as today, but there are many good arguments that municipalities should and can make decisions on reduced shift times locally. When it comes to the proposal on tax-free quotas, which was increased last year, the proposal by the Christian People's Party has been clearer during the comitology process. The Central Party, SV and the Christian People's Party now propose that the government in the 2016 state budget reverse the change in the tax-free regime introduced in 2014. The Labour Party, the Left and the Government Parties vote against. The centre party supports most other proposals from the Christian People's Party, except the proposal to make it legal for municipalities to donate money at a fee for hamlets. We believe that today's arrangement, where there is a cost to be based, is good. We also do not agree with the proposal to impose anti-abuse requirements in order to receive public support for sporting events. It is a commendable proposal, but in the context of national support for major national and international events, it may be difficult. We make an alternative proposal, supported by the Workers' Party and the Christian People's Party, to ask the government to initiate a cooperation with the sports organisations to strengthen the work to make sport a non-alcoholic zone. There has been a debate on the fact that the Labour Party, SV, the Christian People's Party and the Centre repeat in their comments what the government said in the 2012 drug report that consumer product information needs to be limited and that one has to go through the interface between advertising and product information. I think there's a real problem issue in 2015 as in 2012. Moreover, introducing measures that can limit consumer product information does not have to be done by legislative changes. In the food sector, the government has put down a business group that has decided to make a common promise to reduce the salinity of industrial food. I think we need a similar common lift on the alcohol field. The government could take the initiative. The most eye-catching thing in the setting is that, in my view, the Right Party and the Advance Party write that they think it's okay and desirable to use attract prices on alcohol to get young people to visit out-serving earlier this evening. It shows that, despite the consensus of the Committee on the extent of harm and the consequences of alcohol use, there are also considerable differences between the batches in this field. The centre party has a note that points out that over the course of two years, the government has raised several alcohol policy issues that, in some cases, have led to a change in the pace of alcohol policy. In particular, the increase in the tax-free quota has contributed to this. So it's understandable that the Christian People's Party is making a proposal for action for a more solid alcohol policy. By doing so, I propose that we do so.

For those who start early, the risk of developing alcoholism increases. Over 40 pst. alcohol users drank a lot at the end of their youth. Relations and networks are important to work with. The consequences of alcohol are well known – poor health and early death, violence and crime. It's spending a lot of money on us as a society, and especially on health. When the Left has chosen not to vote for any of the proposals, it has to do with the fact that we are four parties that have an agreement that says something about alcohol, and that's the platform. Then, for the autumn, we are going to discuss an escalating plan for the drug field, and I believe that part of the suggestions that have been made here is naturally part of the discussion on the escalation plan. So it's like some of these measures clearly have a budgetary impact, and it's also regulated in the agreement between the four parties. It's not to be awkward or difficult, but we think it'll be a little off the clock to go into the discussions about some other proposal now. Then we think it would be better to have an overall discussion on the escalation plan and in the budget negotiations, where all four parties are sitting at the table. Of course, it's a point to have with the widest possible majority in Stortinget. Now we have the opportunity to discuss some of these points. We have also done so in dealing with the public health communication we are going to discuss later today. Budgetary negotiations are also a process in which all parties are involved. But again – I want to commend the Christian People's Party for being a driver of alcohol policy. We still have something to learn, and everyone can get better. I also believe that with regard to growth policy, some of the structural reforms we are currently engaged in, with both larger municipalities and coordinating several of the small welfare seats in the municipality of Norway, can be involved in the prevention and the relationship creation that we are looking for.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e3443	I agree with the proposers that we need to take active preventive action to reduce alcohol-related harm. I am pleased that the proposers are promoting the voluntary sector and the AKAN as important actors. The rapporteurs and I agree that the main lines of the alcohol policy should be firm. This will include, inter alia: to maintain the authorisation system, the wine monopoly, age limits, fees and advertising ban, as well as a well-functioning follow-up system. The government is taking the challenges of alcohol policy seriously. Prevention efforts are being managed within the framework of public health communication, and efforts for those who are developing or have an alcohol problem are coming back to the escalation plan for the drug field. The notification of the primary health service and the forthcoming health plan are also relevant for this work, and it is also the Youth Health Plan, on health strategy, coming next year. Overall, these documents will show how the government continues its policy on alcohol. We want to keep the main lines because they actually produce results, while also making simplification adjustments to ensure the legitimacy of the policy as a whole. We remove some unnecessary regulation, and inefficient mechanisms should be made more effective. As for my view of each of the 15 proposals that have been put forward, I refer you to my written contribution to the matter. Some of the proposals are proposals that are under evaluation or preparation, some are more or less taken care of, others are proposals that the government opposes. The fact that one is voting against the proposals of the government parties and the cooperation parties should not surprise anyone. I would like to refer to the Stortingen's treatment of this, at the latest at the examination in document 8:29 S on 17 February 2015 and the decision that was made there, which I follow up. With regard to the media's mention of alcohol, I think I have a different approach than the proposers. We would like journalists to write less about alcoholic beverages, but we cannot or should not limit freedom of expression – that is my clear opinion. Advertising disguised as independent publicity is something else. It is already banned today. I would also like to add a few words to the proposal for a change to the advertising ban. The details of the advertising ban are made clear by regulations. Therefore, it is the proposal for regulatory changes that has been out for consultation. I have proposed some changes aimed at meeting the audience's expectations for product information in order to ensure the legitimacy of the advertising ban as a whole. We still want to protect the public from product-driven information. We have therefore assumed that we will only allow information that is given in some form and in a form that requires that information be actively sought. I suggest that regulatory changes can take effect in a few months' time. However, I do not want to hide that the work is technically demanding, and I am concerned that we should work closely with the consultation recordings to ensure a good result. I see that there will always be new borderline cases when you, like here, introduce new exceptions to a general rule. Neither the industry nor the public authorities can expect the adjusted rules to override all interpretations once and for all. Such a result would require an absolute advertising ban or a ban on advertising, and I believe that both of those solutions would not be in anyone's interest.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e3644	First, I would like to thank the rapporteurs for their contribution to putting the palliative issue higher on the political agenda. I would also like to thank the Committee for its very good cooperation in dealing with this important matter. I would like to thank all those who came to the hearing, and not least I would like to say a thousand thanks to all those who work with and engage in the palliative work in Norway. The Committee greatly appreciates the good work done, while listening with interest to recordings about developing the palliative field further. I am therefore pleased that, as a committee, we are raising the palliative field a long step forward, not only with good comments, but also with concrete proposals which, after all, are unanimously adopted here today in the Chamber. The Committee is clear that the palliation field needs to be significantly strengthened in Norway. I would like to quote from the setting: ‘The Committee considers that Norway should have a clear ambition to be the leader in palliative care. In order for this to become a reality, more systematic and planned work needs to be done, willing to learn from other countries, open up different models and thinking in palliative work, strengthen competence at all stages, use more resources, focus on diagnosis neutrality and take into account that palliation in many contexts means more than treatment and care at the end of life.’ This is ambitious, concrete and binding, but the committee sees that you have to think long-term and holistically and turn many stones before you can do this completely. To this end, the Committee calls on the unanimous Government to carry out an overall public investigation, a NOU, of the palliative field, with a broad body of representation from different disciplines, and where children’s palliation is part of the work. The Committee also sees the need to work towards the development of the field in parallel with the NOU work, providing many leaderships in the form of clear comments, as well as two committee proposals dealing with the identification of the need for outpatient child palliative teams linked to all health regions, and looking at the possibilities of advanced home hospitals for children. The Labour Party for its part supports the Christian People's Party's minority proposal No. 1-3, which deals with better organisation for home visits of regular, wide-ranging empowerment in palliation among health professionals and a comprehensively adapted offer for seriously ill children and their families. I would also like to refer to minority motion No. 4 from the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and the Socialist Left Party, dealing with enhanced skills, a better offer to children and the organisation of a variety of offers, including different hospice models. I've said it before, and I'd like to say it again: nothing is more important than this. The whole palliation field needs development. We're going to learn from others. We have to put in place new models, like hospice. We should not at least lift the children's palliative field significantly.
