The exposure, and not least the competence and international networks of this organisation, is significant and is of great value in the further development of children’s palliation in Norway.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e3662	The representative Freddy de Ruiter has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e3665	The NRK gave us a glimpse of soothing care last weekend. We got death close by the gripping story of Ingrid, the woman who in winter, seriously ill, told us about the good relief treatment she received at Hospice Lovisenberg. Unfortunately, last Saturday we heard the family's experience of Ingrid's last days, as they felt little dignity. This is probably sadly the story of the extremes of the soothing care we have in Norway today. It makes a strong impression on all of us when dying patients are afraid to be of concern and are anxious to massage the caregivers. We get sad when relatives feel very insecure. The worry about losing close relatives is great, and it often gets so infinitely heavier if the recent experience is not safe. In the right we have not been satisfied with the care of dying patients. We believe that this field of health care has been down-priority in recent years. Other countries have taken major and important steps to strengthen the palliative field. Hospice philosophy has great impact in many countries. In Norway, you are lucky to meet it in the usual care service. We have asked ourselves whether one may have been too preoccupied with the healing element in the treatment and too little care for people who can no longer be cured. The medical profession has traditionally not had a strong focus on the treatment of dying patients, but we know that both doctors and nurses have strong opinions and a desire to help build a stronger palliative health service in Norway. It is therefore time to strengthen this subject, and I am happy that we have a government that is so clear on this. 15 years after the last review of care for dying patients, our government ordered a technical report on this because there was a need for more knowledge and expertise, both about what we have today, and what we need in the future. I note that today's red-green parties are promoting or supporting many proposals to strengthen palliative care in Norway. It must be allowed to say that the good intentions unfortunately come late from that edge. After their eight years of government, the time was running out for a decisive strengthening of the field. However, we are pleased that an overall health and care committee has listened to important consultations, and that the government is now asked to carry out an overall public investigation, as the rapporteur explained in his statement, a public investigation in which children's palliation is also a natural, integrated part of the work. We are also pleased that a Joint Committee is committed to identifying the need for outpatient teams in all health regions in relation to children's palliation and to exploring the possibilities for advanced home hospitals for children. There are many tasks to be addressed after years of depriving this area. First, we have to give dying patients more choices. That's just 14 pst. dying at home, a very low figure compared to other countries. Half of Norwegian patients die in hospital. We have to make sure that dying and seriously ill people feel that they have several real choices as to where and how they want to spend the last days of life. Secondly, we must ensure that patients in the final phase of life meet with professional competence. Caring for dying patients is demanding; health care requires a high level of competence. Therefore, it is a primary objective of our government to build a professionally strong healthcare service. The decision-makers play an important role, but so do the nurses in the municipalities. It is also the case that health and care managers have a particularly high responsibility for prioritising professional development in the field of care. It is not just patients or relatives who suffer when departments do not drive professional development and empowerment on palliation. We must not underestimate the negative impact of insecurity on health professionals when they have to perform tasks that they do not fully master or have competence. But the most important thing is that we make sure that patients in a vulnerable and difficult situation meet as best they can. In the primary health message presented by the government in the spring, new ideas about how health professionals in municipalities should be organised are announced. We need more teamwork, where multidisciplinary health professionals meet the dying patient. It's just how the different and unique needs of each patient are better met. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e3723	It is a political responsibility to ensure serious ill and dying care and dignity in the final phase of life. The Christian People's Party has proposed a series of necessary measures to promote active life support at the end of life. This initiative is important and can provide an important basis for cross-policy action in the field. At the same time, the Christian People's Party believes that today's situation requires immediate action as well. Existing research provides a good basis for us to implement the most necessary measures that will strengthen the supply of people who are at their most vulnerable. Most people who die spend their last days in the home municipality. Dicent health care providers are particularly vulnerable. Older people experience getting tossed between different levels of care in the last weeks of their lives. The Hospice tanker must be integrated into Norwegian nursing homes. We need to get more doctors in the nursing homes to ensure patients have good pain relief and proper medication at the end of life. The Christian People's Party proposes 600 palliative hospital beds in the municipalities by 2020, which involves sheltered bed units and staff with special competence for care and care at the end of life, both nurses, health professionals, psychologists and priests – those with good competence. Many patients have a desire to spend the last time at home. Much good is done around Health-Norway, but unfortunately there are also examples of poor communication, lack of cooperation between the different levels of care, lack of skills and too few employees, which may make this difficult. The doctor knows the patient over time and has overall responsibility. They must be able to seek out seriously ill patients at home and cooperate closely with home services. Today's system does not provide enough for home visits, and we are therefore pleased that a majority of the committee asks the government to ensure that it is more stimulated to make home visits to the seriously ill and dying. It is estimated that about 3,500 children have palliative diagnosis in Norway today. When a serious illness affects a child, a whole family is affected. Children affected by less-known serious diseases and diagnoses do not have the same access to assistance and organisation as cancer patients with their package schedules, for example. The family shall not have to fight for the necessary treatment, care and relief if the disease of which the child has been affected is less known. When seriously ill children are treated at home, everyday life can be more normalized. The ‘freshest’ activities characterize the day. Several studies point to a reduced level of stress in the child when treatment was given mainly at home and not in the hospital. Hospital stays with sick children are also often very stressful both for parents and for siblings. Advanced home hospitals contribute positively to the overall family health. The Christian People's Party is therefore pleased that a unanimous committee asks the government to investigate the establishment of advanced home hospitals for children associated with all health regions. This offer must be made available to the families concerned, irrespective of their country of residence. The Christian People's Party is also pleased with the commitment to identify the need for outpatient teams for children in all health regions. Teams must have peak competence in children’s palliation, as the existing outpatient teams define children to a small extent as their scope. Teams must be able to visit the child and family at home or in an institution in acute and terminal situations. The Christian People's Party is pleased with the commitment shown in the Committee to ensure a worthy offer to seriously ill and dying, but nevertheless calls for the parties to dare to be offensively committed to further action. Even when serious illness and suffering threaten, the patient, with the right competence and quality of care personnel, can get good treatment and experience quality of life and meaning, even if life goes to the end. This is life help for the dying. Unfortunately, we have a long way to go before this offer is a guarantee for everyone in need. I am promoting with this Christian People's Party's proposal on the matter as well as the proposals we have with other parties.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e3803	People in the final phase of life who need relief and care must have a dignified end to life. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e3944	The Labour Party believes that a regionalised model of management of the specialist health service has proved to respond well to the challenges facing us in the health sector. The debate on substance is closed. 7 earlier today does not trump that position. We have been shown that the introduction of the business model in 2002 has resulted in far more patients being treated in hospitals than in the past. It has provided better quality in patient treatment and has contributed to a good distribution of both economic and professional resources between regions. Through regional superstructure, it has also been possible to raise important local and regional issues through a strong regional player, who has had the opportunity to take overall action on good resource allocation. In particular, this has been important to ensure good quality in local hospitals in regions with large distances and scattered populations. The proposers will have stronger political governance with the hospitals. It now seems clear that a national health and hospital plan cannot be an exhaustive management tool, and we note with some wonder that the government has so far been little concrete about how one really thinks that a national hospital governance model can be. In any event, it is clear that the Minister for Health does not have his words in mind when, in the opposition, he stated that the health plan should be as detailed as the national transport plan: ‘The health plan we are going to draw up will define the tasks of each hospital, also locally, on a national basis.’ Now the health minister says that the plan will be on a very general level and not at all define the tasks of each hospital. It's no wonder that many people are confused about how this government really thinks about the management of hospitals. The Labour Party will maintain a regional governance structure and continuously implement improvements in the management and organisation of Norwegian hospitals. In this way, we will ensure a governance structure that meets the need for local anchoring, people-chosen competence, legitimacy locally and regionally, and broad knowledge of hospital operations and societal issues at all levels of the governance of the specialist healthcare service.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e4002	The health model with the establishment of health care establishments and state ownership of hospitals was introduced in 2002 and is referred to as the main health reform since municipalities were given responsibility for primary health care services in 1984. It has also been said that it is the only major reform in Norway that has been adopted without any prior public spending, and it has been carried out in great haste. From the idea was launched until the laws were passed it took two years. The morbidity reform in 2002 was not just about the state assuming responsibility for hospitals. It was that a whole new operating principle was introduced, away from the management model and beyond, into a business model in line with the private sector. There was strong professional resistance to the reform from the start. Ten emerging countries countered the health care reforms, which are interesting to read now. There they strongly expressed their scepticism for the reform and its consequences. It was the exemption itself from democratic governance that most warns against, because in a market system people in elections or the Storting will have no real influence over health priorities. There was a warning against the bureaucracy wanting to grow and dominate the trade union environment, and there was a warning against trade union developments becoming home because of competition between hospitals, and because the training would become a wolf’s activity. It was warned that over time Norway would have a two-part health care system – one in public ownership and one in private ownership. Sickhouses would be specialised and emergency preparedness would be centralised. The healthiest patients would be given higher priority over the most ill. Very many of these concerns have unfortunately been a reality. The Central Party notes that the Minister of Health is pushing out his will in time and will no longer reject this regime. Now we're going to have a national health and hospital plan, he's going to be adopted, he's going to be implemented, and he's going to work before one says anything more about the board. We want a funding that builds up under a good public administration and equivalent services. We therefore propose that the government should bring forward legislative proposals where health insurance laws are replaced by a new health management law with public authority management of hospitals under state ownership. We ask the government to draw up a detailed national health and hospital plan to ensure political priorities for quality, recruitment, education, functional distribution, location and organisation of specialist services. This plan must lay the framework for elected government regionally and locally. We ask the government through a new health administration law created about 20 health care agencies, similar to the 20 health care agencies of the day, where the majority of the board is elected by and among its members. We ask the government through a new health management law to create state rentals in hospitals that have both professional responsibility and financial and personnel responsibilities. We also propose the creation of a separate works council in hospitals, where the majority are local people elected and where there are representatives from the municipal health services. We also propose that hospitals should be financed mainly within the framework. This means that the order and driver models will be discontinued. The Health and Hospital Plan will outline national principles for different types of hospital and hospital structure. It shall be developed on the basis of the needs of patients, based on up-to-date knowledge and on the plans already developed in healthcare businesses. Once the Storting has addressed the general principles for the development of a comprehensive specialist health service, health authorities shall comply with these principles in professional criteria and quality requirements that can be imposed on hospitals for the future. In cases where governments do not implement adopted policies, the Minister of Health and Health will have to replace the governments in the utmost consequence. This will not only lead to a unclear management model for hospitals, but will also undermine the role of county representatives as elected representatives. One understands that this is a proposal that I will warn very strongly against. It will undermine the role of the Storting as a public elected level responsible for the specialist health service, it will undermine the constitutional responsibility of the Minister of Health and Welfare for the Storting, and it will also undermine the role of the county council members as elected representatives at regional level. The model creates unclear responsibilities and opens up the kind of political games that today's specialist health management model intended to clean up. The design of a future governance model for specialist health services shall support the objective of ensuring that the population is provided with equivalent services of good quality and good use of the resources provided by the Storting. The organisation of the ownership of the specialist healthcare service is one of several instruments to ensure more national policy governance of the sector. This tool must be seen in conjunction with other governance measures that will be presented in the national health and hospital plan. The governance model must ensure good cooperation, coordination and resource use between hospitals and other actors, in particular the health services of municipalities. Patients, users and other interested parties shall be guaranteed influence over the provision of services. The government will come back to Stortinget with this matter after the national health and hospital plan has been submitted.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e4284	In Norway, we have used a wide range of instruments to help reduce alcohol consumption and thereby reduce the harm and problems caused by alcohol use. And, as we were in the previous debate: the wine monopoly is one of the most important alcohol policy instruments we have, and it contributes greatly to limiting the consumption of alcohol. In order to maintain the wine monopoly, it is essential that the wine monopoly be operated in an economically efficient manner. Since lower alcohol consumption and the regulation of alcohol turnover are alcohol policy objectives, the economic performance of the Vinmonopole is not determined – in addition to the requirement for the most cost-effective operation. The Board of Directors of the Vinmonopolet has decided that each store shall bear its own costs. This is an assessment that I support. A prerequisite for maintaining the wine monopoly system is that the scheme has legitimacy and population disintegration. The wine monopoly has a strong upsurge in the Norwegian people: for the third consecutive year, the TNS Gallup wine monopoly peaks its reputation among 59 well-known businesses – well in front of the Flytrain, which came in a fine second place. The wine monopoly will maintain this location and is constantly working to improve operational efficiency and accessibility. The improved availability of the wine monopoly shops has resulted in a strong over-the-counter resolution of the wine monopoly. Today there is a wine monopoly in 232 municipalities, and 90 pst. the population lives in a municipality with its own pole sales. However, I am aware that there were and still are many municipalities that want wine monopoly, which do not have it today. To meet these wishes, we have made sure that the Vinmonopole could develop a new small store model with so-called Category 1 shops. This type of store is especially adapted to small commercial centres and municipalities with few inhabitants. The concept was tested and evaluated in 2014. The evaluation was positive, but also pointed out that there were challenges in establishing such small shops. These shops will have marginal profitability and even small deviations from the established operating mode could result in negative results. Therefore, these sales must have a relatively narrow product range adapted to the customer groups in the area. The wine monopoly is a monopoly company which must have a representative range covering all customer groups. The fact that the Category 1 stores must have short opening hours and employees working part-time will provide lower professional competence, as well as the vulnerability of the store to sickness and absence. Some of this will be compensated for by co-operation with other wine monopolies, but for some Category 1 shops the distances to another pole are so large that they have to be operated as independent units. The evaluation shows that Category 1 shops can be established where there is a market base for 25 400 litres of annual turnover. In November 2014, the Board of Directors decided to open five new stores in this category. This will provide even better accessibility in the districts. I believe it is important to ensure continued good accessibility to the wine monopoly in the districts. However, I believe that ensuring good professional competence and a range of products representing the wide scope of the monopoly is important in order to ensure the opening up of the wine monopoly. In order for this to be possible, there must be a minimum market base for the establishment of a wine monopoly in a municipality. I believe that the establishment of Category 1 shops will enable smaller shops to bear their own costs, while the Vinmonopolet can establish itself in municipalities with few inhabitants.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e4469	The most important thing in health policy is public health. The central party's objectives for public health policy are to improve quality of life, prevent disease and reduce social health inequalities in the population. The government's public health report has many good intentions, but the best thing about the message is that it builds on the cross-political consensus on the overarching framework for public health policy from the previous public health report. The national targets are that Norway should be among the three world's highest-life countries, that the population should experience more years of living with good health and well-being, that social health differences are reduced, and that we should create a society that promotes health in the whole population. Norway has joined the World Health Organization to reduce the premature death of non-infectious diseases such as heart and car diseases, diabetes, coal and cancer by 25 pst. by 2025. The global goal is to stop the increase in diabetes and obesity and at least 10 pst. reduction in the harmful use of alcohol, 10 pst. reduction in the incidence of insufficient physical activity, 30 pst. reduction in the incidence of high blood pressure and 30 pst. reduction in the average intake of salt or sodium. The government writes in the Public Health Communication that they base these goals on national policies and will strengthen efforts across sectors for a more health-friendly lifestyle. I would like to stress that if the policy is to achieve these objectives, then there is a bold policy, because the objectives are quite high, and it will be imperative to achieve them. Such objectives are good, but people's right measures on which I believe this public health message is poor are needed. The government will include mental health in public health work, which the Center Party thinks is a good clarification. Psychiatric disorders are a major health problem, and the social differences are great. We agree with the government that there is a need for better treatment and stronger prevention. For this reason, the Centre has put forward a proposal for a five-year escalation plan for mental health work in the municipalities, which unfortunately was voted down in the Stortinget. Most of the prevention takes place locally. The centre is in favour of developing a programme for public health in municipalities, with a focus on mental health and drug prevention aimed at children and young people. The scheduler writes in the message that they will also facilitate health options. It should be easier to choose healthy. The central party's criticism is that one does not follow this in practice. Facilitate some attempts to increase physical activity in selected youth schools is just not enough to address the major public health challenge when so many young people are too physically active and very sedentary. Cutting out free fruit and green in the school doesn't make the healthy choices easier, it makes the healthy choices harder. It is a fact that, while the previous government's public health report of 2013, underlined universal welfare schemes and health-promoting societies, out-of-date in the 2015 Communication, the new message highlights the individual's possibilities and the commitment to make good choices about his or her health: ‘A society that better facilitates good health choices is a prerequisite for individuals to take more responsibility for their health. The government will make healthy choices simple and natural choices for all.» But there's a clear ideological dividing line here. Universal systems don't just facilitate their own health choices; they contribute to it in concrete terms. When free fruit and greenery were removed, the number of elephants using the scheme went down to 4 pst. This contributes to increased health differences. I could say a lot about different measures, but I want to mention tobacco in particular. It's a very important public health area. It is positive that the Council of State will introduce neutral tobacco packages. The progress party has opposed it, but it does not say anything about it in this setting. The centre party is also critical to include a new tobacco product – e-cigarettes – on the market. With this, I will deal with the proposal of the Centre Party.

Public health – prevention, health promotion – is paradoxically at the same time the most important health work and what is too little priority. It's the treatment we spend the big money on, especially in the last phases of life. We have, as some health researchers have pointed out, even a problem with overtreatment. This shows that we are well protected on the treatment side, but too little attention is paid to the prevention field. So there are major challenges, which Norway is not alone in having, turning resources from treatment to prevention – the tough choices and priorities that need to be made here. Those with higher education or good advice live longer and have several health-healthy years. They feel better and suffer less from mental disorders. It proves to us that public health is closely related to other social conditions. One cannot find one country, not one society, with big differences in income between people who do not at the same time find great health differences. Classifications and health differences are closely linked. The most important struggle that can lead to differences in health is the smooth fight against inequality in society – the class struggle, the struggle to give equal opportunities to all, and not to have too many differences between poor and rich. In such a perspective, tax cuts are particularly severe for the richest. One might say that tax cuts for the richest increase the differences in health between people. There have been some rises, both under the red-green and under this government, but the path to an acceptable standard is still long. Furthermore, we insist on food in the school. Yes, most likely we fight in vain for apple in school, a measure that has a very well documented effect on both health and learning. It is quite unlikely that the Minister of Health did not put down the full-blown foot and insisted that it should be carried on. We have just sent a proposal to introduce food to the school, and we insist on increased physical activity with extended school days. The road to better public health is challenging; it requires conscious choices to reduce social differences and early intervention.

But there are still three important reasons why we have made a new big message about public health. The first is mental health: between one third and one half of the population will experience at least one period of diagnosis of mental illness during their lifetime. Psychiatric health challenges are one of the greatest public health challenges we have today, but the previous message says little about mental health. Being part of the community is as important as cycling to work. Being seen and accepted is as important as fruit and vegetables. Having someone to talk to when you're struggling is as important as quitting smoking. There is a need to prevent better and to ensure that more people receive early assistance. That is why we are now equalising physical and mental health in public health work. Loneliness is one of our major public health challenges. Yeah, it's actually as healthful to be lonely as it's smoking twelve cigarettes a day. Social networking – friends or family – is important for health, quality of life and mastery. We are now taking an initiative for a campaign directed by the voluntary organisations. Through the networks of voluntary organisations, we will mobilise a broad cooperation to prevent solitude. There shall be a low threshold for participation. We will create cooperation between local teams and organisations, and it is important to promote the use of voluntary resources by municipalities and not least to promote a voluntary culture among young people. Facilitating older people to stay longer in the labour market and participate in society is an important part of this government’s public health policy. Retirement reform and raising age limits will help more workers in their 60s to work longer. It is important for the individual, and it is important for us to be able to take care of our welfare society. Now we take the next step and will challenge and engage all social sectors in how they can contribute to more active older people. Last year I took the initiative to set up a business group to participate in improving the diet of the entire population. I'm convinced that most of us want to eat healthier, but the stress in everyday life doesn't always make it easy to choose the healthy option. If the major actors in the food industry agree to develop healthy foods with low levels of salt, sugar and saturated fat, it gives results in the refrigerator to each of us, both in those who think it is easy to live healthy, and in those who find it difficult. Similarly, I want to organize society so that most people can be physically active in their own community and on their own assumptions. Someone likes to go in the mountain, others like to go to the store. Some train ski jumps; others train hip hops. The activity threshold must not be too high. For persons who are not moving much, even a small increase in the level of activity will bring a significant health benefit in terms of reducing disease risk, improving quality of life and improving functioning. I'm concerned that we're passing this on. Taking a walk with Tjukkasgågen is very important, for both physical and mental health. The most important thing is not how you are active, but that you are. Children and young people must be protected separately. It provides protection against violence, intoxication, abuse and care failure. However, it also includes protection against developing harmful behaviour related to tobacco, alcohol, unhealthy diets and physical inactivity. That kind of behavior is always more difficult to change later in life. The Government shall initiate development work with a view to establishing a public health programme. It shall take place in cooperation with the municipalities. The municipalities are close to people's daily lives through universal arenas such as kindergarten, school and the community. The Public Health Act has helped to put public health on the agenda in many municipalities. We're going to reinforce that. The programme shall focus on mental health and alcohol, and children and young people shall be the priority target group. The Public Health Programme will be an instrument for integrating mental health into all local public health work and across the different sectors of the municipalities. Support schemes aimed at children and young people shall be strengthened to ensure that all children have the opportunity to participate in recreational activities. The government's objective is to enable all children to participate in at least one organised leisure activity, regardless of their parents' income and background. I'm very happy that the Storting is busy with increased physical activity in school. Many schools succeed. We see local adaptations and extensive cooperation with sports and outdoor organisations. The core task of the National Centre for Food, Health and Physical Activity is to provide good examples and experiences. In addition, the Centre has been given the task of developing models of how schools and kindergartens can ensure daily physical activity. But the government believes that we need a better knowledge base for future work on body training and physical activity in different parts of the education cycle. This is why we start an experiment with extended time to exercise and physical activity in a range of high school schools. Many of the local initiatives are easy to implement at primary school. There is a considerable amount more demanding in high school. Additional hours at that stage must be filled with good professional content. There is no need for tests to determine whether physical activity is good or not, but there is actually a need for tests to determine how to implement more physical activity in the demanding secondary school stage. This makes this government the largest increase in physical activity in school carried out in modern times — and significantly more than the previous government did. It is particularly important to facilitate good eating and eating habits in children and adolescents. The kindergartens and schools are central arenas, and the government will invite broad cooperation with private and voluntary actors to look at how possible resources, actors and not least measures can support local authorities, which are school owners, working with meals at school. I note that the Committee considers that there is a need to see dietary measures in the context of a comprehensive action plan. This may be useful and could also help to get an overview of the international work to which Norway has joined. In the tobacco field, the aim is to prevent children and young people from starting tobacco, so that we can avoid a new tobacco-dependent generation. It's not about people not having the opportunity to make free choices, but about protecting our children. Smoking and snuffing are harmful to health, and until now I have never met someone who smokes or snuffs who says that he wants children or less siblings to start immediately. The government has consulted on a proposal to introduce standardised tobacco packages, precisely to reduce the attractiveness of tobacco to young people. We will also consider measures to protect tobacco policy from being influenced by the tobacco industry. I am concerned that we need to reach people with drug problems at an early stage, and especially children and young people. That is why we need to catch up with parents with drug problems at such an early stage that one avoids harm to the children and that we can prevent problems from being inherited. For this reason, we will facilitate systematic testing and evaluation of early interventions in the field of drugs — an action we will return to in the escalation plan for the drug field. Children spend much of the day in kindergarten and school, and a good physical and psychosocial kindergarten and school environment should be a matter of course. The government will review and update the legislation on the physical and psychosocial environment of kindergartens and schools.

It is not the tendency to go on social assistance or social security that goes on inheritance, but the underlying causes, and then we have to venture to invest and invest in social work, prevention and early efforts so that the patterns that need to be broken are broken, and people can reach their full length – if not now, then in the next generation.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e4922	The public health report says that solitude is as dangerous as smoking. According to the Central Statistical Office, four out of ten young people aged 16 to 24 and four out of ten aged over 80 say they are lonely. Research says that they risk a worse life and a former death, just as it is for smokers. Therefore, it is not so strange that the Minister of Health has called for a solitude alert. Loneliness is not the same as being socially isolated. Some people actually prefer to be alone – and enjoy it. Loneliness is about being involuntary alone, it's about feelings, it's more about a mismatch between how you feel social, and how you want to have it. Several young people have this spring stood up and shared their stories about how it is to be lonely. One got many ‘likes’ on Facebook for an article when she described her loneliness. Many knew each other. But someone said they didn't dare to 'like' in fear that someone would see them as lonely. It says something about how stigmatizing this is with solitude, and how important it is with more transparency on this topic. When the inability to get out of solitude becomes a permanent state, it goes on the quality of life and well. Then you might need professional help. A deep and long-term involuntary alone situation can lead to mental illness. People who are alone without wishing to do so can become depressed, and we know that more suicides can be linked to loneliness. Several older people — especially those who have become alone — say that they feel lonely and would like to have a more active life and a more social community. An 85-year-old widower said to the magazine We over 60: When you're lonely, you really don't live, you just exist. The government is taking this seriously. In fact, the revised budget proposes 10 million. for measures to combat loneliness. Public health has, to a large extent, been associated with cycling for the job, smoking and salad bar. That is why it is so good that this public health report not only focuses on physical health, but that the communication has such a strong focus on the psychosocial life we live and on the actions that promote mental health. The voluntary sector is an important partner in the fight against loneliness. Loneliness is solved by people meeting and becoming known. Therefore, the good meeting places are important, just as NaKuHel is in my municipality of Asker. There is public health in practice based on the interaction between nature, culture, health and volunteering. NaKuHel is in Asker a door opener for sharing with others. We should all be involved in combating loneliness. It is important that the issue is as clear on the agenda as it is in this Communication. With greater focus, we become more aware that loneliness is a problem for many people. Perhaps we will care a little more, become more attentive and make contact with people in our vicinity who spend a lot of time alone. It's actually one of the best things we can do to fight loneliness.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e4941	This spring I have had the great pleasure of traveling with Trine Skei Grande on a school tour, and we have visited a number of schools and kindergartens. What surprised me a little bit about the tour we've had is that the school health service is important. Not least they say that there are quite small children who have quite big problems. It makes the impression when you're out traveling that way, to hear about the challenges children have — children who don't have to have such challenges. Children who were going to be at school to learn something, to be with their friends and to develop, cannot do it, because they have such big problems either at home, or – as many schools also tell us – it is girls who have so many performance problems, or they feel that they are not doing enough, that they have quite major mental problems as a result of it. That means we have to have a school health service that doesn't spend all its time at internal meetings, but is available at school, which is easy to get access to, and where you can go in there and then, when you need it. I'm glad that the Left Party and the Christian People's Party and the government have lifted the school health service, but I also want to stress that we have a pretty big job ahead of us.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-16.ana.ud1213e4956	In 2014, the Oslo and Akershus College issued a review of social health differences and responses.
